{{ElectionsNY}}
[[Elections]] will be held on November 3, 2026, to elect the 26 [[United States House of Representatives|U.S. representatives]] from the [[New York (state)|state of New York]], one from each of the state's [[New York's congressional districts|congressional districts]]. The elections will coincide with [[2026 United States House of Representatives elections|other elections]] to the House of Representatives, [[2026 United States Senate elections|elections]] to the [[United States Senate]], and various [[2026 United States elections|state and local elections]]. The primary election took place on June 23, 2026. As the state of New York has [[closed primary]] elections, only registered party members are allowed to vote in each party's primary.<ref>{{Cite web|date=May 9, 2025|title=2026 State Primary Election Dates|url=https://www.ncsl.org/elections-and-campaigns/2026-state-primary-election-dates|publisher=National Council of State Legislatures|access-date=August 5, 2025}}</ref>

[[Progressivism in the United States|Progressive]] and [[Democratic socialism in the United States|DSA]] candidates in [[New York City]] had a successful primary night, defeating more establishment and pro-Israel Democratic incumbents in the [[2026 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 10|10th]] and [[2026 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 13|13th]] districts and winning the open [[2026 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 7|7th]] district in a landslide, in what was described as a "clean sweep" and a show of strength for Mayor [[Zohran Mamdani]] and the [[Democratic Socialists of America]].<ref>{{Cite web|title=Clean sweep as 3 candidates endorsed by Mamdani win primaries in New York|url=https://abcnews.com/Politics/clean-sweep-3-candidates-endorsed-mamdani-win-primaries/story?id=134152579|access-date=June 24, 2026|website=ABC News|language=en|date=June 24, 2026|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260624230757/https://abcnews.com/Politics/clean-sweep-3-candidates-endorsed-mamdani-win-primaries/story?id=134152579|archive-date=June 24, 2026|last=Kofsky|first=Jared|last2=Troadec|first2=Halle|last3=Oppenheim|first3=Oren}}</ref><ref>{{Cite news|last=Armus|first=Teo|date=June 23, 2026|title=Mamdani-backed candidates sweep House primaries in NYC. See live results.|url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/elections/2026/06/23/new-york-house-primary-election-live-results-mamdani-candidates-tested/|access-date=June 24, 2026|work=The Washington Post|language=en-US|issn=0190-8286}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web|date=June 24, 2026|title=Clean sweep for Mamdani-backed candidates in New York's Democratic primary|url=https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/clye652m41po|access-date=June 29, 2026|website=www.bbc.com|language=en-GB|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260624130342/https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/clye652m41po|archive-date=June 24, 2026|last=Halpert|first=Madeline|last2=Habeshian|first2=Sareen}}</ref>
{{Horizontal TOC|nonum=yes|limit=2}}

== Failed redistricting attempt ==
Voters in [[New York's 11th congressional district|NY-11]] filed a lawsuit in October 2025, claiming the Staten Island-based district illegally dilutes the power of Black and Hispanic voters in the district.<ref>{{cite web|last=DeJesus|first=Jaime|date=October 28, 2025|title=Lawsuit seeks to redraw Congressional District 11 map, U.S. Rep. Malliotakis responds|url=https://brooklynreporter.com/2025/10/lawsuit-seeks-to-redraw-congressional-district-11-map-u-s-rep-malliotakis-responds/|access-date=October 30, 2025|website=The Brooklyn Home Reporter|language=en-US}}</ref> Acting [[New York Supreme Court]] Justice [[Jeffrey Pearlman]] ordered the [[independent redistricting commission]] to make new maps to reconfigure NY-11 by February 6, 2026.<ref name="NYMap">{{cite web|last1=Ashford|first1=Grace|last2=Oreskes|first2=Benjamin|date=January 21, 2026|title=Judge Rules N.Y. Democrats Can Redraw Staten Island House District|url=https://www.nytimes.com/2026/01/21/nyregion/redistricting-congress-new-york.html|access-date=January 21, 2026|website=The New York Times|language=en-US}}</ref> The deadline was suspended after [[Nicole Malliotakis|Rep. Malliotakis]], who represents the district, appealed the ruling.<ref>{{cite web|last1=Sabella|first1=Lilly|title=The Battle Over New York's 11th Congressional District Lines Has Only Just Begun|url=https://www.thecity.nyc/2026/02/02/congress-redistricting-staten-island-judge-map/|publisher=[[The City (website)|The City]]|access-date=February 4, 2026|date=February 2, 2026}}</ref> On March 2, the U.S. Supreme Court temporarily blocked Pearlman's order, allowing the state's current map to be used for the 2026 election.<ref>{{cite web|last1=Grumbach|first1=Gary|title=Supreme Court blocks redraw of lone GOP-held district in New York City|url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/2026-election/supreme-court-blocks-redraw-lone-gop-held-district-new-york-city-rcna261426|publisher=[[NBC News]]|access-date=March 2, 2026|date=March 2, 2026}}</ref>

{{see also|New York's 1st congressional district}}
The 1st district is based on the eastern end and [[North Shore (Long Island)|North Shore]] of [[Long Island]], including the [[Hamptons]], the [[North Fork (Long Island)|North Fork]], [[Riverhead (town), New York|Riverhead]], [[Port Jefferson]], [[Smithtown, New York|Smithtown]], and [[Shelter Island, New York|Shelter Island]], all in [[Suffolk County, New York|Suffolk County]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Nick LaLota]], who was re-elected with 55.52% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT">{{cite web|url=https://www.cookpolitical.com/vote-tracker/2024/house|title=2024 House Vote Tracker|access-date=December 2, 2024|website=[[The Cook Political Report with Amy Walter|Cook Political Report]]}}</ref>

==District 2==

{{see also|New York's 2nd congressional district}}
The 2nd district is based on the [[South Shore (Long Island)|South Shore]] of [[Suffolk County, New York|Suffolk County]], including the towns of [[Babylon, New York|Babylon]], [[Islip, New York|Islip]], and most of [[Brookhaven, New York|Brookhaven]] all in Suffolk County, and [[Massapequa, New York|Massapequa]] in [[Nassau County, New York|Nassau County]]. The incumbent was Republican [[Andrew Garbarino]], who was re-elected with 59.8% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

== District 3 ==

{{see also|New York's 3rd congressional district}}
The 3rd district is based on the [[North Shore (Long Island)|North Shore]] of [[Nassau County, New York|Nassau County]], including all of [[Glen Cove, New York|Glen Cove]] and [[North Hempstead]], parts of [[Oyster Bay (town), New York|Oyster Bay]], and [[Hempstead, New York|Hempstead]], and the Northeast [[Queens]] neighborhoods of [[Whitestone, Queens|Whitestone]], [[Beechhurst]], [[Little Neck]], and [[Douglaston]].<ref name="validsource">{{cite web|title=Elected Officials & District Map|website=New York State Board of Elections|url=https://www.elections.ny.gov/district-map.html}}</ref>  The incumbent is Democrat [[Tom Suozzi]], who was re-elected with 51.8% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

===Democratic primary===
====Nominee====
*[[Tom Suozzi]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref>{{cite news|last=House|first=Billy|title=LiPetri to pursue rematch against Suozzi after falling short in 2024|url=https://www.newsday.com/long-island/politics/lipetri-suozzi-uxhuwbww|access-date=February 9, 2026|work=Newsday|date=February 3, 2026}}</ref>

====Eliminated in primary====

{{see also|New York's 4th congressional district}}
The 4th district is based on the [[South Shore (Long Island)|South Shore]] of [[Nassau County, New York|Nassau County]] and is entirely within the town of [[Hempstead, New York|Hempstead]]. The incumbent is Democrat [[Laura Gillen]], who flipped the district and was elected with 51.1% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> She is running for re-election.

===Democratic primary===
====Nominee====
*[[Laura Gillen]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref>{{cite news|last1=Booker|first1=Brakkton|last2=Coltin|first2=Jeff|last3=Gardiner|first3=Dustin|last4=Mason|first4=Melanie|last5=Schneider|first5=Elena|title=Kamala Harris has misgivings about government. Some Dems have misgivings about her|url=https://www.politico.com/news/2025/08/02/some-dems-want-kamala-harris-to-sit-midterms-out-00490186|access-date=August 2, 2025|work=[[Politico]]|date=August 2, 2025|language=en|quote=Democratic Rep. Laura Gillen...will not be inviting Harris to visit her district next year as she defends her seat}}</ref>

{{see also|New York's 5th congressional district}}
The 5th district is based in Southeast [[Queens]], including the neighborhoods of [[Jamaica, Queens|Jamaica]], [[Hollis, Queens|Hollis]], [[Laurelton, Queens|Laurelton]], [[Richmond Hill, Queens|Richmond Hill]], [[Ozone Park]], [[Howard Beach]], and [[the Rockaways]]. The district is currently represented by [[Gregory Meeks]], first elected in 1998, who was re-elected with 72.9% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

==District 6==
{{Infobox election

{{see also|New York's 6th congressional district}}
The 6th district is based in Central and Eastern [[Queens]], including the neighborhoods of [[Woodside, Queens|Woodside]], [[Jackson Heights, Queens|Jackson Heights]], [[Elmhurst, Queens|Elmhurst]], [[Kew Gardens, Queens|Kew Gardens]], [[Flushing, Queens|Flushing]], [[Bayside, Queens|Bayside]], and [[Fresh Meadows]]. Incumbent Democrat [[Grace Meng]], who was re-elected with 60.7% of the vote in 2024,<ref name="HPVT"/> is running for re-election.<ref name="Meng11"/>

===Democratic primary===
====Campaign====
Incumbent Congresswoman [[Grace Meng]] had first been elected in [[2012 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 6|2012]], and had built strong political connections with the Queens Democratic Party and with the broader Democratic establishment. She faced a primary attempt from Chuck Park, a former diplomat and political staffer who ran to her left.<ref name=BushwickDaily>{{cite web|url=https://bushwickdaily.com/news/grace-meng-has-held-her-queens-seat-for-13-years-a-former-diplomat-is-trying-to-take-it-tuesday/ |title=Grace Meng has held her Queens seat for 13 years. A former diplomat is trying to take it Tuesday |website=Bushwick Daily |date=June 19, 2026 |accessdate=September 21, 2026}}</ref><ref name=Shekar/>

Park campaigned against Meng's acceptance of donations from the [[American Israel Public Affairs Committee]] (AIPAC), her support for the [[Metropolitan Park (Queens)|Metropolitan Park]] casino project, and her lack of vigor in opposing [[United States Immigration and Customs Enforcement]] (ICE).<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.salon.com/2026/06/04/inside-nycs-overlooked-primary/ |title=Inside NYC's overlooked primary |website=Salon |last1=Payne |first1=Russell |date=June 4, 2026 |accessdate=September 21, 2026}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|url=https://hellgatenyc.com/chuck-parks-hail-mary/ |title=Chuck Park's Hail Mary |website=Hell Gate |last1=Lo |first1=Jasper |date=June 21, 2026 |accessdate=September 21, 2026}}</ref> Meng in turn campaigned on her experience, alleging that Park was more interested in grandstanding than achieving legislative results.<ref name=Shekar/>

====Nominee====
*[[Grace Meng]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref name="Meng11">{{cite news|last1=Reisman|first1=Nick|last2=Sommerfeldt|first2=Chris|title=Mike Lawler's campaign is paying an activist|url=https://www.politico.com/newsletters/new-york-playbook/2026/03/16/mike-lawlers-campaign-is-paying-an-activist-00829417|website=Politico|access-date=March 17, 2026|language=en|date=March 16, 2026}}</ref><ref name="SenLiu">{{cite news|title=Sen. Liu endorses incumbent Rep. Grace Meng in 2026 election|url=https://qns.com/2026/03/liu-endorses-grace-meng-2026-election/|website=Queens Courier|access-date=March 19, 2026|date=March 18, 2026}}</ref>

====Eliminated in primary====
*Chuck Park, former chief of staff for [[New York City Council|New York City council]]member [[Shekar Krishnan]]<ref>{{cite news|last=Kaye|first=Jacob|title=Queens government worker to challenge Meng|url=https://queenseagle.com/all/2025/11/17/queens-government-worker-to-challenge-meng|access-date=November 17, 2025|work=[[Queens Daily Eagle]]|date=November 17, 2025}}</ref>

====Results====
Meng defeated Park by a 14 percent margin; a poor performance for an incumbent and a substantially lower margin than many observers expected.<ref name=NYTAnalysis/> Meng's best areas were those that were majority-white, with the incumbent performing strongly in [[Kew Gardens Hills]], [[Maspeth]] and [[Middle Village, Queens|Middle Village]].<ref name=NYTAnalysis/> Park's best performances were in the neighborhood of [[Woodside, Queens|Woodside]] and among Korean voters in [[Flushing, Queens|Flushing]].<ref name=NYTAnalysis/>

==District 7==

{{see also|New York's 7th congressional district}}
The 7th district is based in [[Brooklyn]] and [[Queens]], including the neighborhoods of [[Clinton Hill, Brooklyn|Clinton Hill]], [[Williamsburg, Brooklyn|Williamsburg]], [[Greenpoint, Brooklyn|Greenpoint]], [[Bushwick]], [[Woodhaven, Queens|Woodhaven]], [[Maspeth]], [[Sunnyside, Queens|Sunnyside]], and [[Long Island City]]. The district is currently represented by Democrat [[Nydia Velázquez]], who was elected with 78.1% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Velázquez is not seeking re-election.

===Democratic primary===
The contest for the 7th district began in November 2025 after longtime incumbent [[Nydia Velázquez]], who had served since 1993, announced her decision to retire.<ref name=Shoes>{{cite web|url=https://www.cityandstateny.com/politics/2025/11/nydia-velazquez-leaves-huge-shoes-who-wants-fill-them/409718/|title=Nydia Velázquez leaves huge shoes. Who wants to fill them?|website=City & State|last1=Alfred|first1=Tsehai|last2=Sterne|first2=Peter|date=November 21, 2025|accessdate=June 27, 2026}}</ref> Velázquez, a longtime reformer who was staunchly opposed to the [[political machine]] associated with the [[Brooklyn Democratic Party]], had cultivated strong ties with the [[New York City Democratic Socialists of America]] (NYC-DSA) in her latter years in office, and with the 7th having a reputation as one of the most left-wing congressional districts in the country it was believed by analysts such as Trip Yang that the organization would seek to have one of its own elected as Velázquez's successor.<ref name=Shoes/>

Velázquez initially remained neutral in the race, a decision attributed by political analyst Michael Lange to her belief that the NYC-DSA would select either state senator [[Julia Salazar]] or New York City Councilor [[Tiffany Cabán]] as their standard-bearer.<ref name="Lange">{{cite web|url=https://www.michaellange.nyc/p/the-not-so-civil-war-for-the-commie|title=The (Not So) Civil War for the Commie Corridor|website=The Narrative Wars|last1=Lange|first1=Michael|date=June 20, 2026|accessdate=June 27, 2026|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260627002145/https://www.michaellange.nyc/p/the-not-so-civil-war-for-the-commie|archive-date=June 27, 2026}}</ref> However, Salazar had no interest in running, and Cabán's brief attempt to was shut down by the newly elected [[mayor of New York]] [[Zohran Mamdani]], a member of the NYC-DSA himself, due to her having been reluctant to support Mamdani's mayoral run.<ref name=Lange/><ref name="Broker">{{cite web|url=https://edition.cnn.com/2026/06/24/politics/zohran-mamdani-democrat-anger-primaries|title=The new power broker: How Zohran Mamdani muscled NYC's Democratic establishment|website=[[CNN]]|first1=Edward-Isaac|last1=Dovere|date=June 24, 2026|accessdate=June 27, 2026|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260624083800/https://edition.cnn.com/2026/06/24/politics/zohran-mamdani-democrat-anger-primaries|archive-date=June 24, 2026}}</ref> Instead, Mamdani and the NYC-DSA selected first-term state assemblywoman [[Claire Valdez]], who had been one of the mayor's earliest and most enthusiastic endorsers, as their candidate.<ref name=Civil/> Velázquez, who had very little relationship with Valdez, viewed this as unacceptable.<ref name=Broker/> She instead threw her support behind Brooklyn [[Borough President]] [[Antonio Reynoso]], an anti-machine progressive who had been one of her longtime protégés.<ref name=Warning/>

Two other candidates, New York City Councillor [[Julie Won]] and public defender Vichal Kumar, also made the primary ballot for the election, but were considered to have no chance of winning.<ref name=Lange/><ref name=Pointers/> While Valdez and Reynoso were ideologically similar, with both wanting to abolish the [[Immigrations and Customs Enforcement]] agency and denouncing the [[Gaza genocide]], their contest quickly become a proxy conflict between the NYC-DSA and the [[Working Families Party]] (WFP), which backed Reynoso.<ref name=Lange/><ref name="Civil">{{cite web|url=https://www.newyorker.com/news/the-lede/can-zohran-mamdani-sway-the-commie-corridors-civil-war|title=Can Zohran Mamdani Sway the Commie Corridor's “Civil War”?|website=The New Yorker|last1=Zhou|first1=Naaman|date=June 18, 2026|accessdate=June 28, 2026|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260623152540/https://www.newyorker.com/news/the-lede/can-zohran-mamdani-sway-the-commie-corridors-civil-war|archive-date=June 23, 2026}}</ref> Michael Lange opined that both organisations were fighting over which would have the leading role in New York City's progressive left, with the insurgent NYC-DSA seeking to supplant the more traditional WFP.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.michaellange.nyc/p/dsa-vs-wfp|title=DSA vs. WFP: Who Leads The Left in New York City?|website=The Narrative Wars|last1=Lange|first1=Michael|date=May 19, 2026|accessdate=June 28, 2026|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260708112336/https://www.michaellange.nyc/p/dsa-vs-wfp|archive-date=July 8, 2026}}</ref>

====Results====
Valdez defeated Reynoso by a margin that was a surprise to observers such as Michael Lange, the state assemblywoman winning by over 20 percentage points.<ref name=Narrative/> Valdez won throughout the district, but her strongest areas were in [[Queens]], especially [[Ridgewood, Queens|Ridgewood]], and the Brooklyn neighborhoods of [[East Williamsburg]] and [[Bushwick, Brooklyn|Bushwick]].<ref name=NYTAnalysis/><ref name=Narrative>{{cite web|url=https://www.michaellange.nyc/p/when-narratives-meet-facts|title=When Narratives Meet Facts|website=The Narrative Wars|last1=Lange|first1=Michael|date=June 29, 2026|accessdate=July 27, 2026}}</ref> Reynoso's only strong performances were in the predominantly Latino neighborhood of [[Cypress Hills, Brooklyn|Cypress Hills]] and among [[Satmar]] voters in [[South Williamsburg]].<ref name=NYTAnalysis/> Such was the scale of Valdez's victory that she even won the neighborhood of [[Los Sures]], which had been Reynoso's place of birth and longtime stronghold.<ref name=NYTAnalysis/>

==District 8==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 New York's 8th congressional district election
| country = New York
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 8

| nominee1 = [[Hakeem Jeffries]]
| party1 = Democratic Party (United States)

| percentage1 =

| alliance2 = Conservative Party of New York State
| nominee2 = Lewis Mizrahi

| before_election = [[Hakeem Jeffries]]

{{see also|New York's 8th congressional district}}
The 8th district is based in Southern and Eastern Brooklyn, including the neighborhoods of Bed-Stuy, Brownsville, East New York, Canarsie, Bergen Beach, Sheepshead Bay, Gravesend, and Coney Island. This district is currently represented by Democrat [[Hakeem Jeffries]], the [[Minority Leader of the U.S. House of Representatives|minority leader]] in the House, who was re-elected with 75.4% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

===Democratic primary===
Incumbent Jeffries had represented the 8th district since 2013, and had been elected  [[House Democratic Caucus#Leaders of the House Democratic Caucus|leader of the House Democratic Caucus]] in 2022 following the retirement of [[Nancy Pelosi]]. Since then he had served as [[House minority leader]] in the face of a Republican-controlled House of Representatives, becoming along with his Senate counterpart [[Chuck Schumer]] one of the most high-profile Democrats during the [[Second Trump administration]].<ref name=Consigliere>{{cite news|url=https://www.nytimes.com/2025/11/14/opinion/hakeem-jeffries-democrats.html|title=Democrats Need a Wartime Consigliere. Hakeem Jeffries Isn't One|newspaper=The New York Times|last1=Cottle|first1=Michelle|date=November 14, 2025|accessdate=June 29, 2026}}</ref> Jeffries had, however, faced criticism, especially from more [[Factions in the Democratic Party (United States)#Progressives|progressive]] Democrats, for the perception that he was a stodgy and awkward opposition figure, unable to adequately oppose congressional Republicans and President Trump.<ref name=Consigliere/>

Seeking to exploit this perceived vulnerability was New York City Councilor [[Chi Ossé]], the youngest member of the Council, who had gained a profile after passing prominent [[tenant rights]] legislation.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/news/2025/11/17/he-won-as-an-insurgent-now-hes-trying-to-stop-one-00654210|title='A real pain': Potential Jeffries primary challenge puts Mamdani in a tough spot|website=Politico|last1=Coltin|first1=Jeff|date=November 17, 2025|accessdate=June 29, 2026}}</ref> Ossé represented [[Bedford-Stuyvesant]] on the Council, an area which was young, [[gentrifying]] and had shown an appetite for insurgent left-wing politics.<ref name=CanHe/> A member of the [[Democratic Socialists of America]] (DSA), Ossé sought to capitalise on the group's recent success in the [[2025 New York mayoral election]] and mobilise them for a primary campaign against Jeffries.<ref name=Hamlet>{{cite web|url=https://www.cityandstateny.com/politics/2025/11/chi-or-not-chi/409617/|title=To Chi or not to Chi?|website=City & State|last1=Donaldson|first1=Sahalie|date=November 18, 2025|accessdate=June 29, 2025}}</ref>

However, his campaign was considered to have almost no chance of succeeding. While Jeffries had faced left-wing criticism for his performance as House Democratic leader he had opposed all major Republican legislation passed during the [[119th Congress]], unlike his Senate counterpart Schumer, and had remained scandal-free.<ref name=Consigliere/><ref name=CanHe>{{cite web|url=https://www.michaellange.nyc/p/can-chi-osse-beat-hakeem-jeffries|title=Can Chi Ossé beat Hakeem Jeffries?|website=The Narrative Wars|last1=Lange|first1=Michael|date=November 19, 2026|accessdate=June 29, 2026}}</ref> He maintained a very strong base of support in the mainly African-American neighbourhoods of [[Canarsie]] and [[East New York]], which had consistently backed moderate candidates in Democratic primaries and which Ossé had not represented in elected office.<ref name=CanHe/> Jeffries also had effectively unlimited resources and institutional support as the House Democratic leader, and left-wing groups were extremely reluctant to endorse against him out of fear of damaging their relationships with the wider Democratic Party.<ref name=CanHe/> These skeptics included some of the most prominent DSA officials in New York City, such as mayor [[Zohran Mamdani]] and U.S. Representative [[Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez]], who actively lobbied the group to not endorse Ossé.<ref name=Hamlet/>

On November 22, 2025, the [[New York City Democratic Socialists of America|New York City branch]] of the DSA voted against endorsing Ossé's campaign, effectively leaving him without the support to continue running.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.cityandstateny.com/politics/2025/11/chi-osse-congress/409723/|title=DSA recommends against endorsing Ossé's congressional bid|website=City & State|last1=Sterne|first1=Peter|date=November 22, 2026|accessdate=June 29, 2026}}</ref> Ossé ended his campaign two weeks later, all but ensuring that Jeffries would win another term in office.<ref name="Wardwell">{{cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/news/2025/12/05/chi-osse-ends-bid-to-challenge-jeffries-00679466|title=Chi Ossé ends early bid to challenge Jeffries|website=Politico|last1=Wardwell|first1=Faith|date=December 5, 2025|accessdate=June 29, 2026}}</ref>

{{see also|New York's 9th congressional district}}
The 9th district is based in South and Central Brooklyn, including the neighborhoods of Prospect Heights, Crown Heights, Midwood, and Borough Park. The district is currently represented by [[Yvette Clarke]], first elected in 2006, who was re-elected with 74.3% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

===Democratic primary===
====Nominee====

====Results====
Clarke comfortably prevailed in the primary, winning nearly 70% of the vote while the remainder was split evenly between Goldfarb and Bristol.<ref name=NYTAnalysis/> Clarke won most precincts throughout the district, but her strongest area of support was in [[East Flatbush, Brooklyn|East Flatbush]], where she lived.<ref name=NYTAnalysis/> Goldfarb's strength was primarily concentrated among [[Hasidic Jews]], allowing him to win numerous precincts in [[Borough Park, Brooklyn|Borough Park]] and [[Midwood, Brooklyn|Midwood]].<ref name=NYTAnalysis/> Despite winning an almost identical number of votes to Goldfarb, Bristol's support was more diffused throughout the district, and he did not win any precincts. His strongest neighborhoods were [[Flatbush]] and [[Crown Heights]].<ref name=NYTAnalysis/>

==District 10==
{{Infobox election

{{see also|New York's 10th congressional district}}
The 10th district is based in [[Lower Manhattan]] and [[Brooklyn]], including the neighborhoods of [[Park Slope]], [[Windsor Terrace]], [[Kensington, Brooklyn|Brooklyn]] [[Gowanus]], [[Brooklyn Heights]], [[Cobble Hill, Brooklyn|Cobble Hill]], [[Red Hook, Brooklyn|Red Hook]], [[Sunset Park, Brooklyn|Sunset Park]], [[Alphabet City]], East Village, the [[Lower East Side]], [[Greenwich Village]], and the [[Financial District, Manhattan|Financial District]]. The district is currently represented by Democrat [[Dan Goldman]], first elected in 2022, who was re-elected with 82.3% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> He faced a primary challenge by former [[New York City Comptroller]] [[Brad Lander]], who positioned himself as the more progressive candidate. Lander won the primary on June 24, 2026.<ref>{{Cite web|last=Solender|first=Andrew|date=June 24, 2026|title=Rep. Dan Goldman unseated by Mamdani-backed Brad Lander|url=https://www.axios.com/2026/06/24/dan-goldman-brad-lander-mamdani-new-york-primary|access-date=June 24, 2026|website=Axios|language=en}}</ref>

===Democratic primary===
{{Undue weight section|date=October 2026|reason=Excessive poorly-sourced information about a disqualified candidate.}}
Goldman, the incumbent representative, was widely viewed as vulnerable to a primary challenge coming into the 2026 electoral cycle.<ref name=PollDan>{{cite web|url=https://www.semafor.com/article/09/10/2025/progressive-poll-mamdani-skeptic-is-vulnerable-in-ny-primary|title=Progressive poll: Mamdani skeptic is vulnerable in NY primary|website=[[Semafor (website)|Semafor]]|last1=Weigel|first1=David|date=September 10, 2025|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref> While generally considered a [[Factions in the Democratic Party (United States)#Progressives|progressive Democrat]], Goldman had only narrowly won the Democratic primary in 2022 with 26% of the vote against a split field of more left-wing candidates, and his re-election campaign in 2024 was considered by analysts such as [[David Weigel]] to have been unimpressive, with the congressman winning only 66% of the primary vote against two low-profile challengers.<ref name=PollDan/><ref name=PoliticoTen>{{cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/news/2025/12/09/brad-lander-set-to-challenge-rep-dan-goldman-from-the-left-00682619|title=Brad Lander set to challenge Rep. Dan Goldman from the left|website=Politico|last1=Ngo|first1=Emily|date=December 9, 2025|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref> Goldman's electoral struggles were attributed in part to his support for [[Israel]], which had become extremely unpopular among left-wing voters as a result of the [[Gaza war]].<ref name=PollDan/>

These left-wing concerns were heightened following the [[2025 New York City mayoral election]]. In the [[2025 New York City Democratic mayoral primary|Democratic primary]] for that election, pro-[[Palestine]] democratic socialist [[Zohran Mamdani]] defeated moderate, pro-Israel [[Andrew Cuomo]] handily, in a performance which included defeating Cuomo by 23 percentage points in the 10th district.<ref name=PoliticoTen/> In the ensuing general election, where Cuomo ran as an independent, Goldman refused to endorse Mamdani due to the latter's pro-Palestine stance.<ref name=PollDan/> Mamdani nevertheless again prevailed, winning the 10th district by 25 percentage points. Goldman's refusal to endorse Mamdani made him an immediate target of the progressive and socialist forces that had backed the incoming mayor, and also showcased the lack of influence he had over voters in his district.<ref name="atlantic-goldman">{{cite web|url=https://www.theatlantic.com/politics/2026/06/goldman-lander-primary-mamdani-democrats/687447/|title=The Liberal District That Could Oust a Trump-Defying Democrat|website=The Atlantic|last1=Berman|first1=Russel|date=June 7, 2026|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref>

Following Mamdani's victory, emboldened progressives began searching for a candidate to challenge Goldman. [[Brad Lander]], the outgoing [[New York City Comptroller|New York City comptroller]] who had finished third in the mayoral primary behind Mamdani and Cuomo and had [[Ranked-choice voting in the United States|cross-endorsed]] Mamdani in the latter stages of the campaign, was considered a strong candidate both because he had wide name recognition from his service as Comptroller and because despite finishing a distant third in the mayoral primary he had performed well in the 10th district itself, where he took 23% of the first-preference vote.<ref name="CityTen">{{cite web|url=https://www.cityandstateny.com/politics/2025/06/could-brad-lander-primary-dan-goldman-ny-10/406430/?oref=csny-skybox-hp|title=Could Brad Lander primary Dan Goldman in NY-10?|website=City & State|last1=Nivokoff|first1=Devyn|date=June 30, 2025|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref> However, Lander was not immediately able to unite the left behind him; NYC Councilor [[Alexa Avilés]] had also been considering a challenge to Goldman and had secured the endorsement of the [[New York City Democratic Socialists of America]] after forming an exploratory committee to do so.<ref name=Learns>{{cite web|url=https://www.cityandstateny.com/politics/2025/11/zohran-learns-early-how-not-please-everyone/409558/|title=Zohran learns early how not to please everyone|website=City & State|last1=Pretsky|first1=Holly|date=November 17, 2026|accessdate=July 6, 2026}}</ref> Mamdani, however, intervened to support Lander, making it known to Avilés that he would endorse the former comptroller if he ran.<ref name=Learns/>

Lander announced that he would be challenging Goldman on December 10, 2025, with Mamdani's backing.<ref name=LanderAnnounce>{{cite web|url=https://edition.cnn.com/2025/12/10/politics/brad-lander-primary-challenge|title=Brad Lander, a Mamdani ally, challenges Rep. Dan Goldman in Democratic primary with mayor-elect's backing|website=[[CNN]]|last1=Pazmino|first1=Gloria|date=December 11, 2025|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref> Perceiving no chance of winning without Mamdani's support, Avilés withdrew the next day.<ref name=LanderAnnounce/><ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.cityandstateny.com/politics/2026/06/rise-tammamdani-hall/414492/|title=The rise of Tammamdani Hall|website=City & State|last1=Sterne|first1=Peter|date=June 29, 2026|accessdate=July 6, 2026}}</ref> Lander immediately became the prohibitive frontrunner, with polls showing him beating Goldman by over 20 percentage points.<ref name=PollDan/> Lander primarily attacked Goldman for not doing enough to oppose the actions of the Trump administration and Israeli government, and for not endorsing Mamdani's general election campaign.<ref name=Hit>{{cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/news/2026/06/24/israel-democratic-party-new-york-primaries-00973287|title=Pro-Israel politics just took a huge hit in New York|website=Politico|last1=Kashinsky|first1=Lisa|last2=Sommerfeldt|first2=Chris|last3=Reisman|first3=Nick|date=June 24, 2026|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref><ref name="atlantic-goldman"/> <!-- And what did Goldman do in response? -->

====Results====
In line with polling, Lander easily defeated Goldman by a margin of over 30 percentage points. His victory was called by media outlets only five minutes after polling stations had closed.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.theguardian.com/us-news/2026/jun/24/brad-lander-democratic-primary-zohran-mamdani|title='Glorious time to be a New Yorker' as Lander's win highlights Mamdani effect|website=The Guardian|last1=Gabbatt|first1=Adam|date=June 24, 2026|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref> Lander easily won the neighborhoods of [[Brownstone Brooklyn]] that had been his longtime base, and also defeated Goldman in [[Lower Manhattan]], which had been the incumbent's base in the 2022 primary.<ref name=NYTAnalysis/> Goldman's only areas of strength were the [[Haredi]] enclave of [[Borough Park, Brooklyn|Borough Park]] and a few richer areas in Lower Manhattan such as [[Battery Park City]], as well as the working-class [[Two Bridges, Manhattan|Two Bridges]].<ref name=NYTAnalysis/>

}}
{{see also|New York's 11th congressional district}}
The 11th district includes all of [[Staten Island]] as well as the [[Brooklyn]] neighborhoods of [[Bay Ridge]], [[Fort Hamilton]], [[Dyker Heights]], [[Bath Beach]], and [[Bensonhurst]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Nicole Malliotakis]], who was re-elected with 64.1% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

A decision by [[Jeffrey Pearlman|a judge]] on [[New York Supreme Court|New York's trial court]] to declare the district's lines [[unconstitutional]] was upheld by the [[New York State Supreme Court, Appellate Division, First Department|intermediate appeals court]] in February 2026.<ref>{{cite news|url=https://www.amny.com/law/appeals-court-green-lights-effort-to-redraw-u-s-rep-nicole-malliotakis-district/|work=AMNY|title=Appeals court green lights effort to redraw U.S. Rep. Nicole Malliotakis' district|first=Max|last=Parrott|date=February 19, 2026|access-date=February 20, 2026}}</ref>

On March 2, 2026, the [[Supreme Court of the United States]] issued a stay blocking implementation of the lower court ruling pending further litigation.<ref>{{cite news|title=Supreme Court blocks redrawing of New York congressional map, dealing a win for the GOP.|url=https://www.wusf.org/2026-03-02/supreme-court-blocks-redrawing-of-new-york-congressional-map-dealing-a-win-for-gop|publisher=NPR|date=March 2, 2026|access-date=April 25, 2026}}</ref>

{{see also|New York's 12th congressional district}}
The 12th district is entirely based in [[Manhattan]], comprising the [[Upper West Side]], [[Upper East Side]], [[Midtown Manhattan|Midtown]], [[Hell's Kitchen]], [[Chelsea, Manhattan|Chelsea]], [[Murray Hill, Manhattan|Murray Hill]], and [[Gramercy Park|Gramercy]]. The incumbent is Democrat [[Jerry Nadler]], who was re-elected with 80.6% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> He is not running for re-election in 2026.

===Democratic primary===
Incumbent Congressman [[Jerry Nadler]], who had served in the House of Representatives since [[1992 United States House of Representatives elections#Special elections|1992]], announced his retirement on September 2, 2025.<ref name=Nadler>{{cite web|url=https://www.theguardian.com/us-news/2025/sep/02/democratic-congressman-jerry-nadler-retires|title=Democratic congressman Jerry Nadler will not seek re-election in midterms|website=The Guardian|last1=Tait|first1=Robert|date=September 2, 2025|accessdate=July 1, 2026}}</ref> Nadler, who was already facing a primary challenge from non-profit executive Liam Elkind, declared that he had decided to retire after witnessing the collapse of the [[Joe Biden 2024 presidential campaign|2024 re-election campaign of Joe Biden]] due to [[Age and health concerns about Joe Biden|concerns over the former president's health]], and that he felt it was time for a younger politician to represent the 12th district.<ref name=Nadler/>

An extremely large field of candidates almost immediately entered the race to replace Nadler.<ref name=YorkerTwelve>{{cite web|url=https://www.newyorker.com/news/the-lede/the-ny-12-primary-is-awash-with-money-but-short-on-belief|title=The NY-12 Primary Is Awash with Money but Short on Belief|website=The New Yorker|last1=Zhou|first1=Naaman|date=June 22, 2026|accessdate=July 1, 2026}}</ref> As the 12th district was safely Democratic, the open seat represented a rare opportunity for local politicians to gain a congressional seat they could not be easily unseated from.<ref name=YorkerTwelve/> Several prominent candidates, such as Elkind, [[New York City Council]]or [[Erik Bottcher]], and left-wing activist [[Cameron Kasky]], initially joined the race, but withdrew well before the primary, as mounting costs and outside spending caused it to become the second-most expensive House primary in history.<ref name="Ngo">{{cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/newsletters/new-york-playbook/2026/01/14/another-shake-up-in-tumultuous-ny-12-race-00726869|title=Another shake-up in tumultuous NY-12 race|website=Politico|last1=Ngo|first1=Emily|last2=Reisman|first2=Nick|date=January 14, 2026|accessdate=July 1, 2026}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.foxnews.com/politics/new-york-congressional-primary-second-expensive-house-race-ever-ai-fight|title=New York congressional primary becomes second most expensive House race ever amid AI fight|website=Fox News|last1=Chu|first1=Bonny|date=June 24, 2026|accessdate=July 1, 2026}}</ref>

As the primary campaign drew on, four main candidates emerged for the seat. These were [[Upper West Side]]  [[New York State Assembly|state assemblyman]] [[Micah Lasher]], [[Upper East Side]] state assemblyman [[Alex Bores]], lawyer [[George Conway]], and [[Jack Schlossberg]], a [[social media influencer]] and member of the [[Kennedy family]].<ref name=YorkerTwelve/> Lasher, who had the endorsement of the outgoing Nadler as well as [[Governor of New York]] [[Kathy Hochul]] and former [[mayor of New York City]] [[Michael Bloomberg]], became widely regarded as the "establishment" candidate in the race despite only being a first-term assemblyman.<ref name=YorkerTwelve/><ref name=TimeTwelve>{{cite web|url=https://time.com/article/2026/06/04/the-chaotic-race-to-represent-manhattan/|title=The Wild Battle to Win Manhattan|website=Time|last1=Terruso|first1=Julia|date=June 4, 2026|accessdate=July 1, 2026}}</ref> Bores, who had co-authored the [[Responsible AI Safety and Education Act]] (RAISE), made strengthening the regulation of [[artificial intelligence]] a large focus of his campaign.<ref name=TimeTwelve/> Conway, formerly a high-profile [[Republican Party (United States)|Republican]] activist, based his campaign entirely around a promise to impeach President [[Donald Trump]] and his cabinet, promising to serve only a single term in office and focus solely on accomplishing this impeachment.<ref name=YorkerTwelve/> Rounding out the four frontrunners, Schlossberg based his campaign around the argument that traditional politics were failing to mobilise young voters under the Democratic Party's banner, and that his large following on social media was evidence that he could inspire them to do so.<ref name=YorkerTwelve/>

Naaman Zhou, writing in ''[[The New Yorker]]'', noted that the ideological differences between the four candidates were minimal; while being interviewed by Zhou, Bores was unable to name a single policy disagreement between Lasher and himself.<ref name=YorkerTwelve/> All four frontrunners supported the continuation of weapons sales to [[Israel]], with only Schlossberg arguing that these sales should be limited to defensive weaponry. The progressive [[Working Families Party]], which had been staunch supporters of Nadler throughout his career, elected not to endorse a candidate, while left-wing activist group [[Our Revolution]] endorsed Bores but was immediately disavowed by the assemblyman for their anti-Israel stance.<ref name=YorkerTwelve/>

In the absence of ideological disagreements, the race soon devolved into a contest of personalities and geographical bases. Due to his passage of the RAISE Act, Bores became the target of significant spending from the artificial intelligence industry, with [[OpenAI]] spending millions of dollars to oppose his candidacy. Bores used this negative spending to argue that voting for his opponents was a vote for unregulated artificial intelligence.<ref name=TwelveDebate>{{cite web|url=https://www.cityandstateny.com/politics/2026/06/ai-and-crypto-take-center-stage-ny-12-debate/413990/|title=AI and crypto take center stage at NY-12 debate|website=City & State|last1=McDonough|first1=Annie|date=June 4, 2026|accessdate=July 1, 2026}}</ref> However, Bores was also supported with millions of dollars by the AI company [[Anthropic]] and by cryptocurrency billionaire [[Chris Larsen]], which led to fierce attacks by Lasher and Schlossberg that if elected Bores would be a puppet of the cryptocurrency industry.<ref name=TwelveDebate/>

Polling in the race was scattershot, with different pollsters showing different candidates in the lead, but the joint attacks by Bores' opponents on his financial backers led ''[[City & State]]''{{'}}s Annie McDonough to argue that Bores was the frontrunner in the race.<ref name=YorkerTwelve/><ref name=TwelveDebate/> Lasher was also considered a likely victor, due to his inheritance of Nadler's Upper West Side base that had already proven victorious over Upper East Side Representative [[Carolyn Maloney]] in the 2022 primary cycle.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.thenation.com/article/politics/micah-lasher-ny-12-democrats-congress/|title=In the Race to Succeed Nadler, Micah Lasher Says Fighting Trump Is Not Enough|website=The Nation|last1=Walsh|first1=Joan|date=June 3, 2026|accessdate=July 1, 2026}}</ref><ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.politico.com/news/2022/08/23/nadler-maloney-new-york-primary-00053435|title=Nadler topples Maloney, ending fellow House Democrat's 30-year tenure|website=Politico|last1=Dunn|first1=Danielle Muoio|last2=Goldenberg|first2=Sally|last3=Shen-Berro|first3=Julian|date=August 23, 2022|accessdate=July 1, 2026}}</ref> Schlossberg's campaign was in contrast marred by disorganization, and critics frequently assailed him for the content of his social media posts, which included a large amount of irreverent material such as [[thirst traps]] that was viewed as unbecoming of a congressional candidate.<ref name=YorkerTwelve/><ref name=TimeTwelve/> Conway was considered to have the least chance of winning, due to his past as a Republican in the heavily-Democratic 12th.<ref name=TimeTwelve/>

====Results====
Lasher won the primary, defeating Bores narrowly. Lasher's primary source of strength was the [[Upper West Side]], which he won comfortably, and he also performed surprisingly strongly on the [[Upper East Side]], winning many precincts that were expected to go to Bores.<ref name=NYTAnalysis>{{cite web|url=https://www.nytimes.com/interactive/2026/06/24/us/elections/nyc-house-primary-results-precinct-map.html|title=See Where Mamdani's Slate of House Hopefuls Did Best|newspaper=The New York Times|last1=Lemonides|first1=Alex|last2=Soler|first2=Caroline|last3=Zhang|first3=Christine|last4=Reynolds|first4=Evan|date=June 24, 2026|accessdate=July 27, 2026}}</ref> Bores' strongest areas were the younger portions of the district, and he won both [[Kips Bay, Manhattan|Kips Bay]] and [[Hudson Yards, Manhattan|Hudson Yards]], alongside [[Midtown Manhattan|Midtown]], but this was not enough to counter Lasher's unexpected strength on the Upper East Side.<ref name=NYTAnalysis/>

{{main|2026 New York's 13th congressional district election}}
{{see also|New York's 13th congressional district}}
The 13th district is based in [[Upper Manhattan]] and the Northwest [[Bronx]], including the neighborhoods of [[Harlem]], [[Morningside Heights]], [[Spanish Harlem]], [[Hamilton Heights]], [[Washington Heights, Manhattan|Washington Heights]], [[Inwood, Manhattan|Inwood]], [[Marble Hill, Manhattan|Marble Hill]], [[Fordham, Bronx|Fordham]], [[Kingsbridge, Bronx|Kingsbridge]], and [[Bedford Park, Bronx|Bedford Park]]. The incumbent was Democrat [[Adriano Espaillat]], who was first elected in 2016, re-elected with 83.5% of the vote in 2024, and was defeated in the Democratic primary by [[Darializa Avila Chevalier]].<ref name="HPVT"/> The primary received national attention as a fight between the Democratic Party's mainstream wing and the party's [[democratic socialist]] wing.<ref name="NYTimes2025King">{{cite news|last1=King|first1=Maya|title=Justice Democrats Re-Emerge in New York to Try to Unseat Espaillat|url=https://www.nytimes.com/2025/11/20/nyregion/justice-democrats-espaillat-avila-chevalier.html|access-date=February 7, 2026|newspaper=[[The New York Times]]|date=November 20, 2025}}</ref><ref>{{cite news|last=Silva|first=Manuela|date=June 19, 2026|title=This New York Race Is a Microcosm of Democrats' Identity Fight|url=https://www.notus.org/2026-election/darializa-avila-chevalier-adriano-espaillat-primary|website=Notus|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20260621222240/https://www.notus.org/2026-election/darializa-avila-chevalier-adriano-espaillat-primary|archive-date=June 21, 2026|access-date=May 12, 2026}}</ref>

====Results====
Avila Chevalier defeated Espaillat in the Democratic primary in an upset.<ref>{{Cite web|date=June 24, 2026|title=Democratic Rep. Adriano Espaillat loses primary to Mamdani-backed Darializa Avila Chevalier|url=https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/2026-election/espaillat-ny-house-primary-loss-district-13-avila-chevalier-rcna351127|last1=Bowman|first1=Bridget|last2=Kamisar|first2=Ben|access-date=June 24, 2026|website=NBC News|language=en}}</ref> The primary electorate was heavily stratified by age, with Avila Chevalier performing best among young voters.<ref name=Narrative/> She won [[Hamilton Heights, Manhattan|Hamilton Heights]], [[Hudson Heights, New York|Hudson Heights]], northern [[Inwood, Manhattan|Inwood]] and both [[Harlem]] and [[Spanish Harlem]].<ref name=Narrative/><ref name=NYTAnalysis/> Espaillat won the [[Bronx]] and the heavily-Dominican [[Washington Heights, Manhattan|Washington Heights]], along with [[Marble Hill, Manhattan|Marble Hill]].<ref name=Narrative/><ref name=NYTAnalysis/>

{{see also|New York's 14th congressional district}}
The 14th district is based in North [[Queens]] and the [[East Bronx]], including the neighborhoods of [[Corona, Queens|Corona]], [[East Elmhurst]], [[Astoria, Queens|Astoria]], [[College Point, Queens|College Point]], [[Hunts Point, Bronx|Hunts Point]], [[Castle Hill, Bronx|Castle Hill]], [[Throggs Neck]], [[Parkchester]], [[Country Club, Bronx|Country Club]], [[Co-Op City]], and [[City Island, Bronx|City Island]]. The district is currently represented by [[Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez]], who was re-elected with 69.2% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

===Democratic primary===
====Nominee====
*[[Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref>{{Cite web|title=The Case For NYC-DSA Endorsing AOC in 2026|url=https://www.groundworkdsa.com/building-up/the-case-for-aoc-2026-nyc-dsa|access-date=May 21, 2026|website=Groundwork|language=en-US}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web|title=Citizen Action of New York Endorses Proven Champions for Working Families in 2026 Re-Election Races|url=https://citizenactionny.org/2026/03/citizen-action-of-new-york-endorses-proven-champions-for-working-families-in-2026-re-election-races/|access-date=May 21, 2026|website=Citizen Action of New York|language=en-US}}</ref>

====Eliminated in primary====
*Marty Dolan, candidate for this district in [[2024 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 14|2024]] and candidate for [[New York City Public Advocate]] in [[2025 New York City Public Advocate election|2025]]<ref>{{cite news|last2=Danuff|first2=Lee|last1=Rosenblum|first1=Tara|title=VOTE 2026: Wall Street investor Marty Dolan launching another primary challenge against Rep. Ocasio-Cortez|url=https://westchester.news12.com/vote-2026-wall-street-investor-marty-dolan-launching-another-primary-challenge-against-rep-ocasio-cortez|access-date=March 10, 2026|work=[[News 12 Westchester]]|date=March 9, 2026}}</ref><ref>{{Cite web|last=Swanson|first=Emily|date=October 8, 2024|title=Marty Dolan, who unsuccessfully challenged U.S. Rep. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez, announces candidacy for NYC public advocate|url=https://www.bxtimes.com/marty-dolan-who-unsuccessfully-challenged-u-s-rep-alexandria-ocasio-cortez-announces-candidacy-for-nyc-public-advocate/|access-date=April 30, 2026|website=Bronx Times|language=en-US}}</ref>
*Felipe Garcia, former business executive<ref>{{cite web|url=https://www.bxtimes.com/hunts-point-native-felipe-garcia-challenging-aoc-in-june-primary/|title=Hunts Point native Felipe Garcia challenging AOC in June primary|website=Bronx Times|last1=Swanson|first1=Emily|date=May 14, 2026|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref>

==District 15==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 New York's 15th congressional district election

{{see also|New York's 15th congressional district}}
The 15th district is based in the [[West Bronx]], including the neighborhoods of [[Mott Haven]], [[Melrose, Bronx|Melrose]], [[Morrisania]], [[Highbridge, Bronx|Highbridge]], [[Tremont, Bronx|Tremont]], [[West Farms, Bronx|West Farms]], [[Belmont, Bronx|Belmont]], [[Norwood, Bronx|Norwood]], [[Woodlawn Heights, Bronx|Woodlawn Heights]], [[Riverdale, Bronx|Riverdale]], and [[Spuyten Duyvil, Bronx|Spuyten Duyvil]]. The district is currently represented by Democrat [[Ritchie Torres]], first elected in 2020, who was re-elected with 76.5% of the vote in 2024. He is running for re-election.

===Democratic primary===
====Nominee====

Ritchie Torres in New York's 15th Congressional District|work=Norwood News|url=https://www.norwoodnews.org/jose-vega-challenges-rep-ritchie-torres-in-new-yorks-15th-congressional-district/|access-date=December 13, 2025|language=en-US}}</ref> ''(running in the general election as a Independent)''

{{see also|New York's 16th congressional district}}
The 16th district is based in southern [[Westchester County]], including [[Yonkers]], [[White Plains, New York|White Plains]], [[New Rochelle]], and [[Rye, New York|Rye]]. It also includes [[Co-op City, Bronx|Co-op City]] in [[the Bronx]]. The incumbent is Democrat [[George Latimer (New York politician)|George Latimer]], who was elected with 71.5% of the vote in 2024.

===Democratic primary===
====Nominee====
*[[George Latimer (New York politician)|George Latimer]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref name="NY2026"/>

==District 17==
{{Infobox election

{{see also|New York's 17th congressional district}}
The 17th district is based in the [[Lower Hudson Valley]], including all of [[Rockland County|Rockland]] and [[Putnam County, New York|Putnam]] counties, northern [[Westchester County]], and a small part of [[Dutchess County]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Mike Lawler]], who was re-elected with 52.2% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/> Lawler is one of only three Republicans in the 119th Congress to represent a district that was carried by Democrat Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential election, making this race a key Democratic target.<ref>{{Cite web|last=Kondik|first=Kyle|title=The 2024 Crossover House Seats: Overall Number Remains Low with Few Harris-District Republicans|url=https://centerforpolitics.org/crystalball/the-2024-crossover-house-seats-overall-number-remains-low-with-few-harris-district-republicans/|date=January 16, 2025|access-date=January 18, 2025|language=en-US}}</ref> Lawler is running for re-election.

The election is one of the most expensive congressional races in the 2026 elections, with over $32.7 million in spending.<ref>{{Cite web |last=Mutnick|first=Ally|date=October 6, 2026|title=The blockbuster House races|url=https://punchbowl.news/article/campaigns/blockbuster-house-races/|access-date=2026-10-15|website=Punchbowl News|language=en-US}}</ref>

{{see also|New York's 18th congressional district}}
The 18th district is based in the mid-[[Hudson Valley]], including all of [[Orange County, New York|Orange County]] and most of [[Dutchess County|Dutchess]] and [[Ulster County|Ulster]] counties. The incumbent is Democrat [[Pat Ryan (politician)|Pat Ryan]], who was re-elected with 57.2% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

== District 19 ==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 New York's 19th congressional district election
| country = New York
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 19

| alliance1 = Working Families Party

{{see also|New York's 19th congressional district}}
The 19th district stretches from the [[Upper Hudson Valley]] across the [[Catskill Mountains]] to parts of the [[Southern Tier]] and [[Finger Lakes]], including [[Hudson, New York|Hudson]], [[Monticello, New York|Monticello]], [[Oneonta, New York|Oneonta]], [[Binghamton]], and [[Ithaca, New York|Ithaca]]. It includes all of [[Columbia County, New York|Columbia]], [[Greene County, New York|Greene]], [[Sullivan County, New York|Sullivan]], [[Delaware County, New York|Delaware]], [[Otsego County, New York|Otsego]], [[Chenango County, New York|Chenango]], [[Broome County, New York|Broome]], and [[Tompkins County, New York|Tompkins]] counties, and parts of [[Rensselaer County, New York|Rensselaer]], [[Cortland County, New York|Cortland]], and [[Ulster County|Ulster]] counties. The incumbent is Democrat [[Josh Riley]], who flipped the district and was elected with 51.1% of the vote in 2024.

{{see also|New York's 20th congressional district}}
The 20th district is based in the [[Capital District (New York)|Capital Region]], including [[Albany, New York|Albany]], [[Troy, New York|Troy]], [[Schenectady]], [[Saratoga Springs]], and [[Amsterdam, New York|Amsterdam]]. It includes all of [[Albany County, New York|Albany]] and [[Schenectady County|Schenectady]] counties, and parts of [[Saratoga County|Saratoga]], [[Rensselaer County|Rensselaer]], and [[Montgomery County, New York|Montgomery]] counties. The incumbent is Democrat [[Paul Tonko]], who was re-elected with 61.1% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

==District 21==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 New York's 21st congressional district election
| country = New York
| type = presidential
| ongoing = yes
| previous_election = 2024 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 21

| before_election = [[Elise Stefanik]]
| before_party = Republican Party (United States)
| after_election = 
| after_party =

{{see also|New York's 21st congressional district}}
The 21st district is based in the [[North Country (New York)|North Country]] and the [[Adirondack Mountains]], and also includes parts of the [[Mohawk Valley]] and the [[Capital District (New York)|Capital District]]. It includes [[Glens Falls, New York|Glens Falls]], [[Lake George (town), New York|Lake George]], [[Plattsburgh, New York|Plattsburgh]], [[Potsdam, New York|Potsdam]], [[Herkimer, New York|Herkimer]], and [[Rome, New York|Rome]]. The incumbent is Republican [[Elise Stefanik]], who was re-elected with 62.1% of the vote in 2024. She is not seeking re-election.

===Republican primary===
====Nominee====
*Anthony Constantino, businessman and candidate for this district in the [[2025 United States House of Representatives elections#Canceled special election for New York's 21st congressional district|canceled 2025 special election]]<ref>{{cite news|last1=Nezaj|first1=Benny|title=Sticker Mule CEO Anthony Constantino launches bid for NY-21 seat|url=https://wnyt.com/top-stories/sticker-mule-ceo-anthony-constantino-launches-bid-for-ny-21-seat/|access-date=November 24, 2025|publisher=[[WNYT]]|date=November 24, 2025}}</ref>

====Eliminated in primary====

== District 22 ==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = 2026 New York's 22nd congressional district election
| country = New York
| type = presidential

{{see also|New York's 22nd congressional district}}
The 22nd district is based in [[Central New York]] and the [[Mohawk Valley]], including [[Syracuse, New York|Syracuse]] and [[Utica, New York|Utica]]. It includes all of [[Onondaga County, New York|Onondaga]] and [[Madison County, New York|Madison]] counties and parts of [[Oneida County, New York|Oneida]], [[Cayuga County, New York|Cayuga]], and [[Cortland County, New York|Cortland]] counties. The incumbent is Democrat [[John Mannion (American politician)|John Mannion]], who flipped the district and was elected with 54.6% of the vote in 2024.

{{see also|New York's 23rd congressional district}}
District 23 is based in the Southern Tier and Western New York, including Elmira, Corning, Jamestown, and outer Erie County. The district is currently represented by Republican [[Nick Langworthy]], who was re-elected with 65.8% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

{{see also|New York's 24th congressional district}}
The 24th district is based along the Lake Ontario coast (minus Rochester) and the upper Finger Lakes, including Watertown, Oswego, Seneca Falls, and Batavia. The incumbent is Republican [[Claudia Tenney]], who was re-elected with 65.7% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

==District 25==

{{see also|New York's 25th congressional district}}
The 25th district is based in the [[Rochester, New York|Rochester]] area, including all of [[Monroe County, New York|Monroe County]] and part of [[Ontario County, New York|Ontario County]]. The incumbent is Democrat [[Joseph Morelle]], who was re-elected with 60.8% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

===Democratic primary===
Incumbent Joseph Morelle had first been elected to the 25th district in [[2018 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 25|2018]] and had been re-elected comfortably since then, with a large financial advantage over his opponents.<ref name=BeaconMorelle>{{cite web|url=https://rochesterbeacon.com/2026/06/23/morelle-coasts-to-victory-in-democratic-primary/|title=Morelle coasts to victory in Democratic primary|website=Rochester Beacon|last1=Ericson|first1=Paul|date=June 23, 2026|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref> He faced two primary challenges in 2026; former [[Brighton, Monroe County, New York|Brighton]] town board member Robin Wilt, who had run against Morelle in the 2018 and 2020 primaries, and pastor Sherita Traywick.<ref name=Moment>{{cite web|url=https://rochesterbeacon.com/2026/03/12/a-race-shaped-by-the-moment/|title=A race shaped by the moment|website=Rochester Beacon|last1=Schermerhorn|first1=Jacob|date=March 12, 2026|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref>

Wilt ran to Morelle's left, accusing him of not adequately opposing the [[Second presidency of Donald Trump|presidency of Donald Trump]] and of being too closely affiliated with the congressional Democratic leadership.<ref name=Moment/> Morelle responded by arguing his House committee assignments made him an effective anti-corruption advocate, and that he had successfully won funds for local infrastructure and businesses.<ref name=Moment/> Traywick campaigned against [[Immigration detention in the second Trump administration|migrant detention centres]] and the [[2026 Iran war]], arguing that new representatives were needed to address these issues.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://spectrumlocalnews.com/nys/rochester/news/2026/06/08/candidates-for-the-25th-congressional-district-seat-speak-out-|title=Democratic candidates for New York's 25th Congressional District seat speak out|website=Spectrum Local News|last1=Wright|first1=Wendy|date=June 9, 2026|accessdate=June 25, 2026}}</ref>

====Nominee====
*[[Joseph Morelle]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref>{{Cite web|title=FEC Form 2 for Report FEC-1854838|url=https://docquery.fec.gov/cgi-bin/forms/H8NY25105/1854838/|access-date=October 6, 2025|publisher=[[Federal Election Commission]]}}</ref>

====Eliminated in primary====
*Sherita Traywick, pastor & candidate for [[New York's 56th State Senate district]] in [[2020 New York State Senate election#District 56|2020]]<ref>{{cite news|title=Sherita Traywick to challenge Joe Morelle in N.Y.'s 25th district|url=https://spectrumlocalnews.com/nys/rochester/news/2026/01/20/sherita-traywick-to-challenge-joe-morelle-in-n-y--s-25th-district-|access-date=January 22, 2026|work=[[Spectrum News]]|date=January 20, 2026}}</ref>
*Robin Wilt, former [[Brighton, Monroe County, New York|Brighton]] Town Board member and candidate for this seat in [[2018 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 25|2018]] and [[2020 United States House of Representatives elections in New York#District 25|2020]]<ref>{{cite news|title=Ex-Brighton Town Board member to primary Joe Morelle|url=https://13wham.com/news/local/ex-brighton-town-board-member-to-primary-joe-morelle|access-date=February 12, 2026|publisher=[[WHAM-TV]]|date=February 12, 2026}}</ref>

{{Infobox election

{{see also|New York's 26th congressional district}}
The 26th district is based in the Buffalo-Niagara Falls area, including the more urban parts of Erie County and western Niagara County. The incumbent is Democrat [[Tim Kennedy (politician)|Tim Kennedy]], who was re-elected with 65.2% of the vote in 2024.<ref name="HPVT"/>

===Democratic primary===
====Nominee====
*[[Tim Kennedy (politician)|Tim Kennedy]], incumbent U.S. representative<ref name="NY2026"/>
